First, it might be enticing to reduce
Lemma

25
to the case where
A_{-}=\emptyset
by observing that
c_{i}=x_{i}-y_{i}
, where
x_{i}
(resp.,
y_{i}
) is the number of vertices in
\bigcup_{e\in\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}}T[e]
that are
i
-reachable from at least one edge of
A_{+}\cup A_{-}
(resp.,
A_{-}
).
However, this reduction cannot be applied here since counters in counter vectors cannot be subtracted.
The technical contribution of
Lemma

25
is to present how
{\bf c}
can be determined using only the operations supported by counter vectors.
Second, even though the sets
A_{+}
,
A_{-}
have total bounded size (
|A_{+}\cup A_{-}|\leq|\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}|\leq 2
), we find it more illuminating to give a proof that proceeds inductively on the size of
A_{+}
.

Proof.

Induction on the size of
A_{+}
.
If
A_{+}=\emptyset
, then the answer is
{\bf 0}
.
Now assume that
|A_{+}|\geq 1
and pick
e\in A_{+}
.
Let
{\bf c}_{\neq e}
be the recursively computed counter vector for the set
A_{+}\setminus\{e\}
, and let
{\bf c}_{e}=N_{v}.\textsc{SumCounters}(e)
.
Observe that a vertex
x\in T[f]
for a cluster edge
f\in\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}
is
i
-reachable from
e
but not from
(A_{+}\cup A_{-})\setminus\{e\}
if all of the following conditions hold:

•

x
is
i
-reachable from
f
;

•

the cover level of the pair
e,f
is at least
i
;

•

the cover level of all pairs
e^{\prime},e
for
e^{\prime}\in(A_{+}\cup A_{-})\setminus\{e\}
is strictly less than
i
. (Equivalently, assuming the previous condition, the cover level of all pairs
e^{\prime},f
is strictly less than
i
.)

The number of vertices
i
-reachable from
f
in
T[f]
is simply
\mathrm{clustercnt}_{f,i}
, and so the number of vertices that satisfy the first two conditions above is
c_{e,i}
.
Then, letting
p
to be the maximum cover level of
e
with all the remaining edges of
A_{+}\cup A_{-}
(which can be determined by
|A_{+}\cup A_{-}|-1
calls
N_{v}.\textsc{Level}(e,\cdot)
), we find that the number of vertices satisfying all three conditions is
c_{e,i}
if
i>p
, or
0
otherwise.

Therefore, the final counter vector
{\bf c}
is precisely
{\bf c}={\bf c}_{\neq e}+[{\bf 0}:(p+1):{\bf c}_{e}]
.
Since
|A_{+}|,|A_{-}|\in\BigO(1)
, it can be verified that the total query time is bounded from above by
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max}))
, plus queries to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost (
2
).
∎

Extending the notation slightly, we say that
N_{v}.\textsc{SumCounters}(A_{+},A_{-})
denotes the operation described in Lemma
25
.
We will frequently have
A_{-}=\emptyset
and in this case we will use the shorthand notation
N_{v}.\textsc{SumCounters}(A_{+})
.

Counters for path clusters

Let
C
be a (slim) path cluster with
\partial C=\{v,w\}
.
We say that a vertex
y\in C
such that
\mathrm{meet}(v,w,y)\notin\{v,w\}
is
(\geq j,i)
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,v}
for
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
if there exists an edge
e\in\pi(C)
incident to
\mathrm{meet}(v,w,y)
such that
c(\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,v},e)\geq j
and
y
is
i
-reachable from
e
.
(Note there are two edges on
\pi(C)
incident to
\mathrm{meet}(v,w,y)
; one separates
y
from
v
, and the other separates
y
from
w
.)
We also say that
y\in C
is
(=j,i)
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,v}
if it is
(\geq j,i)
-reachable but not
(\geq j+1,i)
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,v}
.
Then we define the following counter vectors.

•

\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C}
, where
\mathrm{totalcnt}_{C,i}
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
is the number of vertices
y\in C
such that
y\notin\{v,w\}
and
y
is
i
-reachable from
v\ldots w
;

•

\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C,v}
, where
\mathrm{totalcnt}_{C,v,i}
is the number of vertices
y\in C
such that
y\notin\{v,w\}
and
y
is
i
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,v}
; and
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C,w}
, defined analogously;

•

\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,v,j}
for
j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
, where
\mathrm{diagcnt}_{C,v,j,i}
is the number of vertices
y\in C
such that
y\notin\{v,w\}
and
y
is
(=j,i)
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,v}
.
We also define
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,w,j}
analogously.
Clearly, we can represent
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,v}
and
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,w}
as counter matrices.

For technical reasons, we only store
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C}
explicitly and do not store
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C,\cdot}
or
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,v}
.
Instead, we maintain the counter matrices
\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v}
and
\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,w}
that represent the corresponding values of
\mathbf{diagcnt}
in
C

before
the application of pending lazy updates, if any, to
C
.
Then the corresponding counter matrices
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,v}
,
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,w}
representing the state of
C

after
applying these updates can be determined “on the fly” from
\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v}
as:

\mathrm{diagcnt}_{C,v,j,i}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }j\leq\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}\text{ and }j\neq\mathrm{cover}_{C},\\
\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}}\mathrm{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v,j^{\prime},i}&\text{if }j=\mathrm{cover}_{C},\\
\mathrm{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v,j,i}&\text{if }j>\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}.\end{cases}

Also we have that
\mathrm{totalcnt}_{C,v,i}=\sum_{j=i}^{\ell_{\max}}\mathrm{diagcnt}_{C,v,j,i}
.
Observe thus that both
\mathbf{diagcnt}
and
\mathbf{totalcnt}
can be computed from
\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}
in a constant number of counter vector and matrix operations, which takes time
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max})\log\ell_{\max})
by
Lemma

6
.

Computing counters

The values of all counters described above are computed for a cluster during a merge.
For leaf clusters, we can see that all counter vectors are identically zero.
We now distinguish cases on how a cluster
C
splits into child clusters
A
,
B
(and possibly a point cluster
P
).

•

If
\partial A=\partial B=\partial C=\{v\}
(case
(1)
in
Figure

2
), no counters need to be computed or updated.

•

If
\partial A=\{v,x\}
,
\partial B=\{x\}
,
\partial C=\{v\}
(case
(2)
), then
C
is the deepest cluster in the top tree that entirely contains
T[\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v}]
; hence the vector
\mathbf{clustercnt}_{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v}}
should be updated.
Recall that
\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{x}=\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\}
.
Let
{\bf c}^{x}=N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})
, so that
c^{x}_{i}
is the number of vertices
y\in\bigcup_{\vec{xx^{\prime}}\neq\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}}T[\vec{xx^{\prime}}]
that are
i
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}
.
Then we can compute
\mathrm{clustercnt}_{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v},i}
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
as the sum of the following values:

–

\mathrm{totalcnt}_{A,v,i}
(the number of vertices
y
with
\mathrm{meet}(v,x,y)\notin\{v,x\}
that are
i
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v}
);

–

1+c^{x}_{i}
if
\mathrm{cover}_{A}\geq i
(the number of vertices
y
with
\mathrm{meet}(v,x,y)=x
that are
i
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v}
).
The
+1
summand comes from the vertex
y=x
.

Hence
\mathbf{clustercnt}_{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v}}
can be computed from
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{A,v}
and
{\bf c}^{x}
in a constant number of counter vector operations.

•

If
\partial A=\{v,x\}
,
\partial B=\{x,w\}
,
\partial P=\{x\}
and
\partial C=\{v,w\}
(case
(3)
), then we first calculate counter vectors:

–

{\bf c}^{AB}=N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\})
;

–

{\bf c}^{A}=N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})
(and
{\bf c}^{B}
analogously);

–

{\bf c}^{B\setminus A}=N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\},\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})
(and
{\bf c}^{A\setminus B}
analogously).

Noting that
\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{x}=\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}
,
\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\}
, we can compute
\mathrm{totalcnt}_{C,i}
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
as the sum of:

–

\mathrm{totalcnt}_{A,i}
,

–

\mathrm{totalcnt}_{B,i}
,

–

1+c^{AB}_{i}
.

(These values represent the number of vertices
y
that are
i
-reachable from
v\ldots w
and such that
\mathrm{meet}(v,w,y)
is, respectively: strictly between
v
and
x
, strictly between
x
and
w
, and equal to
x
.)
Hence
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C}
is computed from
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{A}
,
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{B}
, and
{\bf c}^{AB}
within a constant number of counter vector operations.

Then, letting
\ell_{AB}
be the cover level of the pair of edges
\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}
,
\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}
(i.e.,
\ell_{AB}=N_{x}.\textsc{Level}(\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x})
), we compute
\mathrm{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v,j,i}
as the sum of the following values:

–

\mathrm{diagcnt}_{A,v,j,i}
,

–

\mathrm{diagcnt}_{B,x,j,i}
if
\min\{\mathrm{cover}_{A},\ell_{AB}\}>j
,

–

\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{\ell_{\max}}\mathrm{diagcnt}_{B,x,j^{\prime},i}
if
\min\{\mathrm{cover}_{A},\ell_{AB}\}=j
,

–

1+c^{A}_{i}
if
\mathrm{cover}_{A}=j
and
\ell_{AB}<j
,

–

1+c^{AB}_{i}
if
\mathrm{cover}_{A}=j
and
\ell_{AB}\geq j
,

–

c^{B\setminus A}_{i}
if
\mathrm{cover}_{A}>j
and
\ell_{AB}=j
.

(Again, the values
\mathrm{diagcnt}_{A,v,\cdot,\cdot}
; the values
\mathrm{diagcnt}_{B,v,\cdot,\cdot}
; and the values
(1+)c^{\star}_{i}
represent the number of vertices
y
that are
i
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v}
and such that
\mathrm{meet}(v,w,y)
is, respectively: strictly between
v
and
x
; strictly between
x
and
w
; and equal to
x
.)

For efficiency, we also observe that
\mathrm{diagcnt}_{A,v,j,i}=0
for
j<\mathrm{cover}_{A}
. This allows us to express the sum of
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{A,v}
and the part of
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{B,x}
for
j<\min\{\mathrm{cover}_{A},\ell_{AB}\}
as the result of a matrix splicing operation
\llbracket{\mathbf{diagcnt}_{B,x}}\,:\,{\min\{\mathrm{cover}_{A},\ell_{AB}\}}\,:\,{\mathbf{diagcnt}_{A,v}}\rrbracket
and avoid an expensive element-wise addition of matrices.
This enables us to compute
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,v}
in a constant number of counter vector and matrix operations; see
Section

B.3
for technical details.
Then
\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,w}
is computed analogously.

Time complexity of a merge

By Lemma
6
, for any path cluster
D
and
v\in\partial D
, both
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{D,v}
and
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{D,v}
can be computed on the fly from
\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{D,v}
in
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max})\log\ell_{\max})
time.
After this computation, we can assume access to these values in child clusters
A
,
B
of
C
.

In each case of the merge, we call
N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}
a constant number of times, each time with some set of interface edges for
x
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
.
Hence by Lemma
25
, all these calls take total time
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max}))
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO\left(\sum_{e\in\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{x}}\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(x)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(e)}\right)
.

If
\partial A=\{v,x\}
,
\partial B=\{x\}
,
\partial C=\{v\}
(case
(2)
), the counter vector
\mathbf{clustercnt}_{e}
is computed from vectors
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{A,v}
and
{\bf c}^{x}
in a constant number of vector operations in time
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max}))
.
Moreover, we call
N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})
and
N_{v}.\textsc{UpdateCounters}(\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v},\cdot)
.
By
Lemmas

25
and
4
, these take total time
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max}))
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO(\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C))
.

Finally, for the case
\partial A=\{v,x\}
,
\partial B=\{x,w\}
,
\partial C=\{v,w\}
(case
(3)
), the counter vector
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C}
is computed in a constant number of vector operations from
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{A}
,
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{B}
and
{\bf c}^{AB}
. And
\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v}
is determined in a constant number of matrix and vector operations from
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{A,v}
,
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{B,x}
,
{\bf c}^{A}
,
{\bf c}^{B}
and
{\bf c}^{AB}
.
This again can be done in time
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max})\cdot\log\ell_{\max})
.
Additionally, we call
N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}
a constant number of times, which takes time
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max}))
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO(\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C))
.

Summing up, a single merge of
C
takes time
\BigO(T(\ell_{\max})\cdot\log\ell_{\max})
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO(\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C))
.
Therefore, by the Transient Expose Lemma (
Lemma

20
), each transient expose and unexpose takes additionally time
\BigO(\log n\cdot T(\ell_{\max})\log\ell_{\max})
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO(\log n)
.

Resolving
\textsc{FindSize}(p,q,i)

Armed with the set of counters defined above, it is straightforward to determine the answer to the size query: “
count the number of vertices of
T
that are
i
-reachable from any edge on the path
p\ldots q
”.
Namely, upon a transient expose of the path
p\ldots q
, we hold a transient top tree
\mathcal{U}
representing the tree
T
.
Recall that
\mathcal{U}
consists, in fact, of three root clusters: a slim path cluster
C_{pq}
with
\partial C_{pq}=\{p,q\}
and two point clusters
C_{p},C_{q}
with
\partial C_{p}=\{p\}
,
\partial C_{q}=\{q\}
.
We now find the result of
\textsc{FindSize}(p,q,i)
to be the sum of the following values:

•

\mathrm{totalcnt}_{C_{p,q},i}
: the number of vertices
y
with
\mathrm{meet}(y,p,q)\notin\{p,q\}
that are
i
-reachable from
p\ldots q
;

•

1+\left(N_{p}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{C_{p,q},p}\})\right)_{i}
: the number of vertices
y
with
\mathrm{meet}(y,p,q)=p
that are reachable from
p\ldots q
, including
y=p
;

•

1+\left(N_{q}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{C_{p,q},q}\})\right)_{i}
: the analogous count of vertices
y
satisfying
\mathrm{meet}(y,p,q)=q
.

Afterwards, we restore the original set of exposed vertices.
It is easy to see that the whole process requires one transient expose and transient unexpose and additionally time
\BigO(\log n)
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO(\log n)
.
This satisfies the requirements of
Lemma

24
.

4.5
Implementing
FindFirstReach
and
FindStrongReach

We now move to the implementation of
FindFirstReach
and
FindStrongReach
.
Recall that in
\textsc{FindFirstReach}(p,q,i)
, we have to return a tuple
(ab,c,y)
, where
ab\in E(p\ldots q)
with
a
closer than
b
to
p
,
c\in\{a,b\}
and
y
is an
i
-marked vertex
i
-reachable from
p\ldots q
at
ab
through
c
. Ties are broken by first minimizing the distance between
p
and
a
in
T
, and then minimizing the distance between
p
and
c
.
On the other hand, in
\textsc{FindStrongReach}(p,q,e,b,i)
, we are required to find an
i
-marked vertex
y
that is
strongly

i
-reachable from
p\ldots q
at
e
through
b
.
(In both cases,
\bot
is to be returned when no such
i
-marked vertex exists.)

As previously, we assume that
T
is represented by a top tree
\mathcal{T}
, and the weights of the edges in the neighborhood data structures are defined with respect to
\mathcal{T}
.
Again, at the start of each query, we transiently expose
\{p,q\}
, causing
\mathcal{T}
to be temporarily replaced with another top tree
\mathcal{U}
, but preserving the weights of the edges in the neighborhood data structures as defined with respect to
\mathcal{T}
.

Both types of queries will be resolved in two phases.
First, we will determine whether the answer (a sought
i
-marked vertex
y
) exists.
In the positive case, we will additionally determine the values of
a,b,c
(in case of
FindFirstReach
), as well as a
container
of
y
.
A container will be a nonempty set
Y
of vertices with the property that every
y\in Y
is a correct answer to a query at hand.
A container will be of one of the following types:

•

an
explicit container
of the form
Y=\{y\}
;

•

an implicitly-defined
cluster edge container
, defined by a cluster edge
\vec{vw}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
, so that
p,q\notin T[\vec{vw}]
and
Y
is the set of
i
-marked vertices
y\in T[\vec{vw}]
that are
i
-reachable from
vw
;

•

an implicitly-defined
path cluster container
, defined by a tuple
(C,v,w)
, where
C
is a path cluster of
\mathcal{U}
with
\partial C=\{v,w\}
and
E(C)\cap E(p\ldots q)=\emptyset
, so that
Y
is the set of
i
-marked vertices
y\in C
such that
\mathrm{meet}(v,w,y)\notin\{v,w\}
and
y
is
i
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,v}
.

In the first case, we can immediately resolve the query by returning
y
.
Otherwise, a second phase is necessary in which we will recursively
refine
the given container to produce an example vertex
y\in Y
.
We delay the implementation of this refinement scheme to
Section

4.6
; there, we will prove the following statement.

Lemma 26
.

Given an implicitly-defined container
Y
, we can determine a vertex
y\in Y
in worst-case time
\BigO(\log n)
, plus queries to the neighborhood data structures of the form
Level
,
SelectedLevel
,
OrMarks
,
FindMarked
of worst-case total normalized cost
\BigO(\log n)
.

Assuming
Lemma

26
, we will now prove the following statement.
Recall from
Section

2.1
that
B(\ell_{\max})
denotes the time required to perform an operation on a bit vector and that
B(\ell_{\max})\leq T(\ell_{\max})
.

Lemma 27
.

The data structure
CL
from
Lemma

22
can be extended to a restricted tree cover level data structure
FR
additionally supporting
Mark
,
Unmark
,
FindFirstReach
and
FindStrongReach
so that:

•

Each transient expose and unexpose takes additionally worst-case
\BigO(\log n\cdot B(\ell_{\max})\log\ell_{\max})
time, plus calls to
UpdateMarks
and
OrMarks
in the neighborhood data structures of worst-case total normalized cost
\BigO(\log n)
.

•

Mark
and
Unmark
takes worst-case constant time, plus one transient expose and unexpose.

•

FindFirstReach
and
FindStrongReach
takes worst-case
\BigO(\log n)
time, plus one transient expose and unexpose, and calls to
Level
,
OrMarks
and
FindMarked
in the neighborhood data structures of worst-case total normalized cost
\BigO(\log n)
.

The implementation of
FindFirstReach
and
FindStrongReach
phase will follow in the most part the blueprint of
FindSize
, so in this description we will omit some proofs and implementation details that are immediate adaptations of their counterparts in
Section

4.4
.
We refer the reader to
Section

B.4
for the pseudocode of the operations described in this section.

From now on we assume that the neighborhood data structures implement the marking extension.

Bit vectors for neighborhood data structures

For every cluster edge
\vec{vw}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
we store a bit vector
\mathbf{ismarked}_{\vec{vw}}
.
For
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
, the value
\mathrm{ismarked}_{\vec{vw},i}
specifies whether any
i
-marked vertex in
T[\vec{vw}]
is
i
-reachable from
vw
.
We assume that
\mathbf{ismarked}_{\vec{vw}}={\bf 0}
for interface edges
\vec{vw}
.
The vector
\mathbf{ismarked}_{\vec{vw}}
is the mark vector
{\bf b}^{vw}
stored in the neighborhood data structure
N_{v}
, hence whenever
\mathbf{ismarked}_{\vec{vw}}
is updated, it is stored in
N_{v}
via the call
N_{v}.\textsc{UpdateMarks}(vw,\mathbf{ismarked}_{\vec{vw}})
.

In order to facilitate the implementation of
FindStrongReach
, we need to store an additional
shifted
vector in the neighborhood data structure
N_{v}
.
Let
\mathbf{ismarked}^{+}_{\vec{vw}}
denote the shifted version of
\mathbf{ismarked}_{\vec{vw}}
, where
\mathrm{ismarked}^{+}_{\vec{vw},i+1}=\mathrm{ismarked}_{\vec{vw},i}
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}-1\}
and
\mathrm{ismarked}^{+}_{\vec{vw},0}=0
.
Then we have a copy
N^{+}_{v}
of the neighborhood data structure
N_{v}
which stores, for each edge
\vec{vw}
,
\mathbf{ismarked}^{+}_{\vec{vw}}
as the mark vector
{\bf b}^{+,vw}
.

We now adapt
Lemma

25
to the setting of bit vectors, producing the following two statements:

Lemma 28
.

Let
v\in V(T)
.
Then, for any
A_{+},A_{-}\subseteq\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}
we can determine a bit vector
{\bf b}
such that, for every
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
,
b_{i}
denotes whether there exists an
i
-marked vertex in
\bigcup_{e\in\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}}T[e]
that is
i
-reachable from any edge in
A_{+}
, but not
i
-reachable from any edge in
A_{-}
.
The query takes worst-case time
\BigO(B(\ell_{\max}))
, plus queries to the neighborhood data structures of worst-case total normalized cost
\BigO\left(\sum_{e\in A_{+}\cup A_{-}}\left(1+\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(v)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(e)}\right)\right)
.

Proof.

In the absence of the set
A_{-}
, the result of the query would simply be a bitwise-or of bit vectors of the form
{\bf c}_{e}\coloneqq N_{v}.\textsc{OrMarks}(e)
for
e\in A_{+}
.
However, if
A_{-}
is nonempty, these bit vectors must be zero-truncated similarly to the proof of
Lemma

25
: for each
e\in A_{+}
, let
p_{e}
be the maximum cover level of
e
with the edges of
A_{-}
, and
{\bf d}_{e}\coloneqq[{\bf 0}:(p_{e}+1):{\bf c}_{e}]
.
Then, adapting the argument from the proof of
Lemma

25
, we find that
d_{e,i}
denotes whether there exists a vertex in
\bigcup_{e\in\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}}T[e]
that is
i
-reachable from
e
, but not
i
-reachable from any edge in
A_{-}
.
Hence the result of the query is actually the bitwise-or of all vectors
{\bf d}_{e}
.
It is straightforward to verify the time and normalized cost bounds of the implementation of the query.
∎

Lemma 29
.

Let
v\in V(T)
.
Then, for any
A\subseteq\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}
and
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
, we can determine a cluster edge
\vec{vx}\in\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}
(respectively,
\vec{vx^{+}}\in\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}
) such that there exists an
i
-marked vertex
y\in T[\vec{vx}]
(resp.,
y\in T[\vec{vx^{+}}]
) that is
i
-reachable (resp., strongly
i
-reachable) from some edge of
A
; we place
\vec{vx}=\bot
(resp.,
\vec{vx^{+}}=\bot
) if such an edge does not exist.
The query takes worst-case time
\BigO(1)
, plus queries to the neighborhood data structures of worst-case total normalized cost bounded by the sum of:

•

\BigO\left(\sum_{e\in A}\left(1+\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(v)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(e)}\right)\right)
; and

•

\BigO\left(\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(v)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{vx})}\right)
(resp.,
\BigO\left(\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(v)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{vx^{+}})}\right)
) if the query returns an edge
\vec{vx}
(resp.,
\vec{vx^{+}}
).

Proof.

Naturally,
\vec{vx}
is found by iterating all
e\in A
and querying
N_{v}.\textsc{FindMarked}(e,i)
.
If at least one of these operations returns an edge as a result, we forward it as the result of our query.
Similarly,
\vec{vx^{+}}
is determined by performing queries of the form
N^{+}_{v}.\textsc{FindMarked}(e,i+1)
.
The entire process takes time
\BigO(1)
, plus queries to the neighborhood data structures of required total normalized cost.
∎

We extend the interface of the neighborhood data structure
N_{v}
so that
N_{v}.\textsc{OrMarks}(A_{+},A_{-})
returns the vector
{\bf b}
described in the statement of
Lemma

28
.
Then
N_{v}.\textsc{FindMarked}(A,i)
denotes the operation of
Lemma

29
returning the cluster edge
\vec{vx}
, and
N^{+}_{v}.\textsc{FindStrongMarked}(A,i)
returns the cluster edge
\vec{vx^{+}}
.
Moreover, we define that
\BigO\left(\sum_{e\in A}\left(1+\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(v)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(e)}\right)\right)
is the
base cost
of a call to
FindMarked
(resp.,
FindStrongMarked
), and the optional summand
\BigO\left(\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(v)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{vx})}\right)
(resp.,
\BigO\left(\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(v)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{vx^{+}})}\right)
) is the
recovery cost
of the call.

Bit vectors maintained by the data structure

For each vertex
v
of the tree we maintain a bit vector
\mathbf{bmarks}_{v}
such that
\mathrm{bmarks}_{v,i}
denotes whether the vertex
v
is
i
-marked.

Also, for each (slim) path cluster
C
with
\partial C=\{v,w\}
, we store the following bit vectors.

•

\mathbf{totalmarks}_{C}
, where
\mathrm{totalmarks}_{C,i}
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
is true whenever there exists an
i
-marked vertex
y\in C\setminus\{v,w\}
that is
i
-reachable from
v\ldots w
;

•

\mathbf{totalmarks}_{C,v}
, where
\mathrm{totalmarks}_{C,v,i}
is true whenever there exists
y\in C\setminus\{v,w\}
that is
i
-reachable from
\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,v}
; and
\mathbf{totalmarks}_{C,w}
, defined analogously;

•

\mathbf{diagmarks}_{C,v,j}
and
\mathbf{diagmarks}_{C,w,j}
for
j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell_{\max}\}
which are the bit vector analogs of
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,v,j}
and
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,w,j}
.

These vectors can be computed analogously to their counter counterparts during both transient and permanent merges, and it can be easily verified that the time required to compute all these vectors during a single merge of
C
is bounded by
\BigO(B(\ell_{\max})\cdot\log\ell_{\max})
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO(\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C))
.
Hence by the Transient Expose Lemma (
Lemma

20
), each transient expose and unexpose takes additionally time
\BigO(\log n\cdot B(\ell_{\max})\log\ell_{\max})
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO(\log n)
— as required.

Updates via
Mark
and
Unmark

Suppose a vertex
u
is to be
i
-marked or
i
-unmarked.
We begin by transiently exposing the vertex
u
, temporarily producing a top tree
\mathcal{U}
.
Observe that after the expose,
u
is not part of any subtree
T[\vec{vw}]
for a cluster edge
\vec{vw}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
; and moreover, the marks on
u
do not influence any bit vectors or bit matrices stored together with path clusters of
\mathcal{U}
.
Hence marking or unmarking
u
resolves to updating contents of
\mathbf{bmarks}_{u}
.
Afterwards, the vertex
u
is transiently unexposed.
Obviously, this all takes constant time, plus a single transient expose and unexpose.

Implementing
FindFirstReach
(p, q, i)

Following the blueprint laid down by
FindSize
, we first perform a transient expose on
\{p,q\}
, producing a transient top tree
\mathcal{U}
representing
T
.

\mathcal{U}
has three root clusters that partition
E(T)
: a (slim) path cluster
C_{pq}
with
\partial C_{pq}=\{p,q\}
and two point clusters
C_{p}
,
C_{q}
such that
\partial C_{p}=\{p\}
,
\partial C_{q}=\{q\}
.

We first test if the sought
y
is a vertex of
C_{p}
, or equivalently, we test if
a=c=p
.
Observe that this is true precisely when either
p
is
i
-marked (in which case we return an explicit container
\{p\}
), or
f\coloneqq N_{v}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{C_{pq},p}\},i)\neq\bot
(in which case we return an implicit cluster edge container defined by
f
).
In both positive cases, we have
ab=\mathrm{firstedge}_{C_{pq},p}
and
c=p
.

Otherwise, we check if a sought
y
belongs to
C_{pq}
, but is neither
p
nor
q
(equivalently,
c\neq q
holds).
This is true if and only if
\mathrm{totalmarks}_{C,i}
holds.
In this case, we will determine a container for
y
via the following recursive auxiliary function:

\textsc{FindInPath}(C,v,w)
: Let
C
be a (slim) path cluster such that
\pi(C)=v\ldots w\subseteq p\ldots q
.
Find a tuple
(ab,c,Y)
such that
ab\in\pi(C)
,
c\in\{a,b\}\setminus\partial C
and
Y
is a container for an
i
-marked vertex
y
that is
i
-reachable from
\pi(C)
at
ab
through
c
.
In case of ties, minimize
\mathrm{dist}(a,v)
first and then minimize
\mathrm{dist}(c,v)
.
We assume that the sought
y
exists in
C
.

We implement
FindInPath
as follows.
Since
C
is a (slim) path cluster (and not a leaf of
\mathcal{U}
since otherwise the sought
y
cannot exist), it splits into two slim path clusters
A
,
B
and a point cluster
P
according to case
(3)
of
Figure

2
.
Suppose that
\partial A=\{v,x\}
,
\partial B=\{x,w\}
and
\partial P=\{x\}
.
We perform the following steps in order.

1.

Test if the sought
y
is in
A\setminus\partial A
; this is true if and only if
\mathrm{totalmarks}_{A,i}
holds.
In this case, the answer to the query is
\textsc{FindInPath}(A,v,x)
.

2.

Test if the sought
y
is in
P
. This holds either if
x
is
i
-marked (in which case we return
(ab,c,Y)=(\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},x,\{x\})
), if
f_{A}\coloneqq N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x})\neq\bot
(and then we return
(\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},x,f_{A})
, where
f_{A}
defines an implicit cluster edge container), or if
f_{B}\coloneqq N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x})\neq\bot
(and then we return
(\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x},x,f_{B})
).

3.

Test if the sought
y
is in
B\setminus\partial B
; this holds if and only if
\mathrm{totalmarks}_{B,i}
holds.
Then, the answer to the query is
\textsc{FindInPath}(B,x,w)
.
Note that this test must succeed due to the assumption that the sought
y
belongs to
C
.

Finally, if
y
belongs to neither
C_{p}
nor
C_{pq}\setminus\{p,q\}
, we verify if
y
can be found in
C_{q}
.
This is done analogously to the case of
C_{p}
: either
q
is
i
-marked (and we return
(\mathrm{firstedge}_{C_{pq},q},q,\{q\})
), or
f\coloneqq N_{v}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\mathrm{firstedge}_{C_{pq},q},i)\neq\bot
(and then we return
(\mathrm{firstedge}_{C_{pq},q},q,f)
).

Time analysis of
FindFirstReach

A single transient expose is performed.
Aside from this, the time complexity of the query is dominated by recursive calls to
FindInPath
.
Observe that
FindInPath
is invoked with slim path clusters
C
lying on a vertical path in
\mathcal{U}
.
Each recursive call performs calls to
N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}
a constant number of times, with arguments from
\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{x}
, where
x
is the midpoint of
C
.
By
Lemma

29
, such calls have base cost
\BigO\left(\sum_{e\in A}\left(1+\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(v)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(e)}\right)\right)
, or equivalently
\BigO(\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C))
.
Moreover, the recursion is short-circuited as soon as any invocation of
FindMarked
returns an edge.
In other words, across all recursive calls in total, at most one call to
FindMarked
incurs additional recovery cost (and this cost is obviously bounded by
\BigO(\log n)
).
Hence by the
Item

4
of the Transient Expose Lemma (
Lemma

20
), the total normalized cost of calls to the neighborhood data structures is bounded by
\BigO(\log n)
, as required.
As announced, the second stage (refinement of implicitly-defined containers) is deferred to
Section

4.6
; but assuming it can be performed efficiently (
Lemma

26
), we conclude that the time requirements of
Lemma

27
hold in case of
FindFirstReach
.

Implementing
\textsc{FindStrongReach}(p,q,e,b,i)

Firstly, we transiently expose the vertices
p,q
, yielding a temporary top tree
\mathcal{U}
.
After the expose, the set of edges with tail
b
that do not lie on the path
p\ldots q
is precisely
\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{b}
.
Hence it is enough to figure out if there exists a cluster edge
\vec{bx^{+}}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
with tail
b
such that there is some
i
-marked vertex
y\in T[\vec{bx^{+}}]
that is strongly
i
-reachable from
e
.
This can be then done by calling
N_{b}^{+}.\textsc{FindStrongMarked}(\{e\},i)
, which has normalized cost at most
\BigO(\log n)
by
Lemma

29
.
If the return value is
f\neq\bot
, we return
y
in the form of an implicit cluster edge container defined by
f
.
After the query, we transiently unexpose
p,q
.

Plainly, assuming
Lemma

26
, the entire process takes time
\BigO(\log n)
, plus a single transient expose and unexpose.

4.6
Refining implicit containers

If
FindFirstReach
or
FindStrongReach
returns an implicit container
Y
in lieu of
y
, we need to perform the second stage of the query: refine
Y
to produce an explicit vertex
y\in Y
to return as the result of the query.
This refinement should be efficient so as to meet the time requirements of
Lemma

26
.
We shall implement the refinement through the following methods:

•

\textsc{RefineClusterEdgeContainer}(\vec{vw},i)
: Refine a cluster edge container defined by a cluster edge
\vec{vw}\in\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{v}
;

•

\textsc{RefinePathClusterContainer}(C,v,w,i)
: Refine a path cluster container defined by
(C,v,w)
, where
C
is a slim path cluster in
\mathcal{U}
.

The implementation of these methods will be mutually recursive.
They will altogether implement a scheme of recursive descent on the (transiently exposed) top tree
\mathcal{U}
, successively progressing through the clusters of the top tree lying on a single root-to-leaf path in the top tree.
This will allow us to argue that each of these helper functions is efficient.

For
\textsc{RefineClusterEdgeContainer}(\vec{vw},i)
, let
C
be the (unique) point cluster of the
\mathcal{U}
such that
\partial C=\{v\}
and
vw
is the unique edge of
C
incident to
v
.
Then,
C
splits into a path cluster
A
and a point cluster
B
according to case
(2)
in
Figure

2
, so that
\partial A=\{v,x\}
and
\partial B=\{x\}
.
The result to the query is now found via the following case study.

•

If
\mathrm{totalmarks}_{A,v,i}
is true, then a sought vertex
y
exists in
A
and we can recursively return
\textsc{RefinePathClusterContainer}(A,v,x,i)
.

•

Otherwise, the path from
v
to sought
y
must pass through
x
. Therefore, it must be the case that
\mathrm{cover}_{A}\geq i
(otherwise no such vertex
u
can exist). Then, if
x
is itself
i
-marked, we can immediately return
x
as the result.

•

Otherwise, there must exist some cluster edge
f\neq\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}
with tail
x
such that the sought
y
is in
T[f]
.
Since
\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{x}=\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\}
, such
f
will be found by letting
f\coloneqq N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\},i)
.
Then, we return
\textsc{RefineClusterEdgeContainer}(f,i)
recursively.

For
\textsc{RefinePathClusterContainer}(C,v,w,i)
, recall that
C
splits into two path clusters
A
,
B
and a point cluster
P
(case
(3)
in
Figure

2
).
Letting
\partial A=\{v,x\}
,
\partial B=\{x,w\}
,
\partial P=\{x\}
, we have
\mathrm{interface}^{\mathcal{U}}_{x}=\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\}
.
We perform another case study:

•

If
\mathrm{totalmarks}_{A,v,i}
holds, some sought vertex
y
exists in
A
; and moreover, this vertex satisfies
\mathrm{meet}(v,c,u)\notin\{v,c\}
. Hence we can return
\textsc{RefinePathClusterContainer}(A,v,c,i)
.

•

Otherwise, the path between
v
and the sought
y
must contain
x
, so it must be the case that
\mathrm{cover}_{A}\geq i
. Again, if
x
itself is
i
-marked, we immediately return
x
as the result.

•

Otherwise, a sought
y
exists in
P
if and only if
T
contains a cluster edge
f\in\mathrm{cluster}^{\mathcal{U}}_{x}
such that
y
is in
T[f]
; so the cover level of the pair
f,\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}
must be at least
i
, and
y
must be
i
-reachable from
f
.
Such
f
is found — if it exists — by letting
f\coloneqq N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\},i)
.
If
f\neq\bot
, we return
\textsc{RefineClusterEdgeContainer}(f,i)
.

•

Otherwise, a sought
y
must exist in
B
; in particular, the cover level of the pair
\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}
must be at least
i
, and
\mathrm{totalmarks}_{B,x,i}
must hold.
This means that we can recursively return
\textsc{RefinePathClusterContainer}(B,x,w,i)
.

The implementation of these functions can be found in
Section

B.4
.

Time analysis

We first observe that the recursion descends along clusters on a single root-to-leaf path in
\mathcal{U}
.
This is straightforward: if
C,C^{\prime}
are two clusters of
\mathcal{U}
pertaining to two consecutive recursive calls (either the slim path cluster itself in case of a path cluster container, or the point cluster
T[\vec{vw}]
in case of a cluster edge container defined by a cluster edge), then
C^{\prime}\subsetneq C
by a simple case analysis.
Each of the two recursive functions performs a constant number of inspections of bits in bit vectors, and a constant number of calls of the form
N_{x}.\textsc{Level}
and
N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}
(where
x
is the midpoint of the cluster
C
pertaining to the recursive call).
Thus:

•

Excluding the calls to the neighborhood data structures, each recursive call takes time
\BigO(B(\ell_{\max}))
per visited cluster.

•

When a cluster
C
is examined, the calls to
N_{x}.\textsc{Level}
have normalized cost
\BigO(\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C))
, and the calls to
N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}
have base cost
\BigO(\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C))
.
Hence by
Item

4
of the Transient Expose Lemma (
Lemma

20
), the sum of these costs is
\BigO(\log n)
.

•

It remains to bound the total recovery cost of calls to
N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}
.
Suppose we call
N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}
when considering a cluster
C
, and the call returns a cluster edge
\vec{xy}
.
This call incurs additional recovery cost bounded by
\BigO(\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(x)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{xy})})
, and immediately afterwards, we recursively return
\textsc{RefineClusterEdgeContainer}(\vec{xy},\cdot)
.
So let
C^{\prime}
be the (unique) point cluster of
\mathcal{U}
with
\partial C^{\prime}=\{x\}
, where
xy
is the unique edge of
C^{\prime}
incident to
x
.
Then
C^{\prime}
splits into child clusters according to case
(2)
and so, by definition,
\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C^{\prime})\geq 1+\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(x)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathrm{firstedge}_{C^{\prime},x})}>\log\frac{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(x)}{\omega_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{xy})}
.
Thus the recovery cost of the call is upper-bounded by
\mathrm{ncost}_{\mathcal{T}}(C^{\prime})
.
Therefore, since all clusters
C^{\prime}
lie on a single vertical path in
\mathcal{U}
,
Item

4
of the Transient Expose Lemma applies and the sum of recovery cost of calls to
N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}
across all recursive calls is also bounded by
\BigO(\log n)
.

Therefore the entire recursion takes time
\BigO(\log n\cdot B(\ell_{\max}))
, plus calls to the neighborhood data structures of total normalized cost
\BigO(\log n)
.
Consequently, our implementation meets the time complexity bounds postulated by
Lemma

26
.

5
Neighborhood structure

This section is dedicated to the proof of Lemma
4
. Our proof is phrased as, first, a description of a data structure, followed, then, by the pseudocode for using and updating the data structure upon queries and updates. The pseudocode is annotated with running times of each line, most of which are straightforward to see from the description of the data structure. There are a few exceptions, where the running times need a brief argument; these are isolated in
Claims

30
,
31
,
32
and
33
.

In general, we will analyse everything as if we had single values in the leaves. However, in the end, the leaves will hold counter and bit vectors of length
\ell_{\max}
, and thus, we will spend an extra factor of
T(\ell_{\max})
time on each operation, where
T(\ell_{\max})
is the time needed to perform operations on an
\ell_{\max}
-sized vector.

Description of data structure

Let
X_{\mathrm{sel}}\subseteq X
be the set of at most
2
selected
edges. Then instead of maintaining the collection of nested sets

\{\mathcal{L}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\max}]}
explicitly, we maintain a slightly
modified collection of nested sets
\{\mathcal{L}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\max}]}

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{i}(v)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}_{i}^{\prime}(v)&\text{if $i=-1$ or $\lvert\mathcal{L}_{i}(v)\cap X_{\mathrm{sel}}\rvert\leq 1$}\\
\mathcal{\cup}_{x\in X_{\mathrm{sel}}}\mathcal{L}_{i}^{\prime}(x)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

In other words, for
i\geq 0
every set in
\mathcal{L}_{i}
that contains both selected edges is the disjoint union of
2
sets in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}
.

We represent the neighborhood data structure as a rooted tree, where
the leaves are the elements of the set
X
, and all leaves have depth

\ell_{\max}+1
. Each internal node at depth
i+1
corresponds to a set in

\mathcal{L}_{i}^{\prime}
. In particular the root corresponds to the set
X

where
\mathcal{L}_{-1}^{\prime}=\{X\}
. Together with a variable
representing
SelectedLevel
this is sufficient to reconstruct

\{\mathcal{L}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\max}]}
. The main benefit of representing
the pair
(\mathcal{L}^{\prime},\textsc{SelectedLevel})
instead of
\mathcal{L}

directly, is that it makes
\textsc{LongZip}(
) and
\textsc{LongUnzip}(
) run in worst-case
constant time since their preconditions ensure that only

SelectedLevel
needs to be changed.

Let
\{s_{1},s_{2}\}=X_{\mathrm{sel}}
be the selected edges. We call a
node
X_{\mathrm{sel}}
-marked
if it is an ancestor of
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
,
and
X_{\mathrm{sel}}
-unmarked
otherwise; we will explicitly store in
each node which (if any) of
s_{1},s_{2}
it is ancestor to. Note that this is unrelated to the “marking” extension that we will cover later in
Section

5.1
. Also observe that we have introduced an arbitrary ordering between
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, which we use for disambiguation below.

Solid-path decomposition

To get the remaining operations to have the desired cost, we use a
version of
heavy-light decomposition
similar to
[
34
,
22
]
. Define the weight of the
subtree
T_{v}
rooted at a node
v
representing some set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{i}^{\prime}
as
w(T_{v})=\sum_{s\in S}w(s)
. Call an edge
(c,p)
between a
child
c
and a parent
p

heavy
if either 1)
c
is

X_{\mathrm{sel}}
-marked and an ancestor of
s_{1}
; or 2)
c
is
X_{\mathrm{sel}}
-marked and

p
is not an ancestor of
s_{1}
; or 3)
p
is not
X_{\mathrm{sel}}
-marked and the
total weight
w(T_{p})<2w(T_{c})
. If
(c,p)
is heavy we will say that
c
is a
heavy child
. Edges that are not heavy are
called
light
and if
(c,p)
is a light edge, then we say that
c
is a
light child
. Since each node has at most one heavy
child, the heavy edges form a system of
heavy paths
.

Suppose we mark each edge in the tree as either
solid
or
dashed
, in such a way that for every node at most one of its child edges is solid. Then the solid edges form a system of
solid paths
. We will maintain the invariant that between operations on the neighborhood structure, an edge is solid if and only if it is heavy. However, this invariant may temporarily be broken during operations.

We can represent each solid path using a balanced binary search tree
(ignoring the weights). Since the height of the original tree is

\ell_{\max}
, the height of this binary search tree is
\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
,
and we can safely assume that concatenating or splitting solid paths
only touches a prefix of this rooted tree of size
\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
. These trees will help us in querying about various information, for example the highest vertex of a solid path (to traverse the tree efficiently), weights of subtrees (to efficiently determine heavy/light children and maintain the heavy paths structure) or the deepest vertex of a solid path with some prescribed property.
inline, color=blue!25,
inline, color=blue!25,
todo:

inline, color=blue!25,

J:
E.g. the data needed to do
FindMarked
correctly.

Each node can have many dashed children, and we store the set of these children using the
almost biased binary tree
implemented by
the
biased disjoint sets
structure of Section
6
(Theorem
5
). The root of this set for vertex
v
will be called
v.\textrm{dchildren}
. After each external query to the neighborhood data structure, the set represented by
v.\textrm{dchildren}
will correspond exactly to the set of light children of
v
, however in the intermediate states of computations, heavy children may at times be a part of this set too. That includes children that are ancestors of selected edges and it is easy to modify the structure to handle the at most two selected edges separately, giving them constant depth.
Otherwise, for a dashed child
c
of a node
p
the depth of
c
in this structure
is
\BigO(\log\frac{w(T_{p})}{w(T_{c})}+\log\log w(T_{p}))
. By Theorem
5
, this depth is both the worst-case bound on the time required to follow the parent pointers from the leaf of an almost biased binary tree corresponding to a dashed child (
Find
), and the amortized cost for deleting a dashed child from the biased disjoint sets structure (
Delete
). Note that if
c
is a heavy child of
p
, but is not an ancestor of a selected edge, then
\log\frac{w(T_{p})}{w(T_{c})}
is a constant and its depth is
\BigO(\log\log w(T_{p}))
.

The
light depth
of a node
v
is the number of light edges on
the path from
v
to the root
r
, and is easily seen to be at most

\big\lfloor\log_{2}\frac{w(T_{r})}{w(T_{v})}\big\rfloor+2
.
Thus, the path from
v
to
r
intersects at most
\big\lfloor\log_{2}\frac{w(T_{r})}{w(T_{v})}\big\rfloor+3
heavy paths.

We will ensure that the similarly-defined
dashed depth
is always
\BigO\big\lparen 1+\log\frac{w(T_{r})}{w(T_{v})}\big\rparen
, thus the path from
v
to
r
intersects at most
\BigO\big\lparen 1+\log\frac{w(T_{r})}{w(T_{v})}\big\rparen
solid paths, and the sum of the depths in the dashed-child structures for
v
telescopes to

\BigO((1+\log\frac{w(T_{r})}{w(T_{v})})\cdot\log\log w(T_{r}))
.

The total depth of a node
v
– understood as the sum of the depths of the parts representing a subtree containing
v
in all dashed-child and solid-path structures – is therefore
\BigO((1+\log\frac{w(T_{r})}{w(T_{v})})\cdot(\log\log w(T_{r})+\log\ell_{\max}))
.

The tree structure will be accessed and updated via several operations:

•

\textsc{Parent}(x)
returns the parent of
x
in the tree;

•

\textsc{UnselectHeavy}(x)
assumes the solid child (if any) is heavy and makes it dashed. Similarly
\textsc{SelectHeavy}(x)
assumes that
x
has no solid child edge and makes heavy child of
x
(if any) solid. Additionally, they pass all incurred updates to the biased disjoint sets data structure and update heavy path trees. Note that these are similar to, and implemented in terms of, the
slice
and
splice
operations from
[
34
,
22
]
.

•

\textsc{Expose}(x)
and
\textsc{Conceal}(r)
modifies the system
of solid paths. We follow the terminology from
[
22
]
and
say that a tree where edges are solid if and only if they are
heavy is called
proper
. A tree where there is a solid path
with the root of the tree as its topmost vertex and
x
as its
bottommost vertex, and where every node not on this path has its
solid child set to its heavy child (if any), is called

x
-exposed
or just
exposed
.
\textsc{Expose}(x)

takes a proper tree containing node
x
and makes it
x
-exposed
and returns the root
r
.

\textsc{Conceal}(r)
takes an exposed tree with given root
r
and
makes it proper.

It follows from
[
22
]
combined with our data structures
for dashed children and solid paths that
\textsc{Expose}(x)
and

\textsc{Conceal}(r)
both take
\BigO((1+\log\frac{W}{w(T_{x})})\cdot(\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max}))
time.

•

\textsc{Link-Exposed}(x,p)
, where
x
is the root of a proper
tree and
p
is a node in a (different)
p
-exposed tree, adds
x

as a child of
p
, leaving the resulting tree

p
-exposed. Conversely
\textsc{Cut-Exposed}(x)
, where
x
has
parent
p
and the tree is
p
-exposed, unlinks
x
from its
parent
p
in the tree, leaving the tree containing
p
as

p
-exposed and the new tree with root
x
proper. Since these
operations only update the dashed children of a single node with
no solid child, and on the auxillary information on the exposed
root path they can be implemented to take

\BigO(1+\log\frac{\max\{w(T_{p}),w(T_{p}^{\prime})\}}{w(T_{x})}+\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max})
time.

•

\textsc{Link}(x,p)
adds
x
as a child of
p
and
\textsc{Cut}(x)
unlinks
x
from its parent in the tree.

\textsc{Link}(x,p)
can be trivially implemented as
r\leftarrow\textsc{Expose}(p)
,
\textsc{Link-Exposed}(x,p)
and
\textsc{Conceal}(r)
in
\BigO((1+\log\frac{W}{w(T_{x})}+\log\frac{W}{w(T_{p})})\cdot(\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max}))
time. Similarly
\textsc{Cut}(x)
can be implemented as
p\leftarrow\textsc{parent}(x)
,
r\leftarrow\textsc{Expose}(p)
,
\textsc{Cut-Exposed}(x)
,
\textsc{Conceal}(r)
, also in

\BigO((1+\log\frac{W}{w(T_{x})}+\log\frac{W}{w(T^{\prime}_{p})})\cdot(\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max}))
time.

Note that if
p
is the root of its tree, or is
X_{\mathrm{sel}}
-marked,
both operations take just
\BigO(\log\frac{W}{w(T_{x})}+\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max})
time, and if furthermore
p
has no other children
it takes just
\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
time.

Annotated pseudocode

In the pseudocode below, we explain how we obtain the running times stated in Lemma
4
, by annotating each line of the pseudocode with its running time. Most of these are relatively straightforward to see, but there are a few which require a short argument, and those, we now outline as claims.

Claim 30
.

When
\textsc{Parent}(x)
returns
y
, it takes time
\BigO(\log\frac{w(y)}{w(x)}+\log\log W)
.

Proof.

If
x
is a heavy child, it has a direct pointer to its
parent
y
, and
\textsc{Parent}(x)
takes
\BigO(1)
time. Otherwise

x
is a light child of
y
, and stored as an element in a biased
disjoint sets tree whose root has a direct pointer to
y
. So in
this case
y
is the pointer stored in the node returned by

\textsc{Find}(x)
, and the running time follows directly from the
running time for
Find
in Theorem
5
.
∎

Claim 31
.

The query to the nearest common ancestor of
x
and
y
, which we write as
x\bot y
, takes time
\BigO((1+f(x)+f(y))\cdot(\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max}))

where
f(z)
is
1
if
z\in X_{\mathrm{sel}}
, and
f(z)=\log\frac{W}{w(z)}
otherwise.

Proof.

We use
\textsc{Expose}(y)
to make
y
the end of a heavy root path in
\BigO((1+f(y))\cdot(\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max}))
amortized time. Then traverse up the
\BigO(1+f(x))
light edges (via
Parent
) and heavy paths (by traversing the binary search trees storing the paths) until hitting the path containing
y
. The total cost of this traversal is
\BigO((1+f(x))\cdot(\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max}))
.
∎

Claim 32
.

Level ancestor of a selected node takes time
\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
. For any other node
x
, level ancestor takes time

\BigO((1+\log\frac{W}{w(x)})\cdot(\log\ell_{\max}+\log\log W))
.

Proof.

Use
\textsc{Expose}(x)
, and find the
i
th element in the resulting heavy path.
If
x
is a selected node,
\textsc{Expose}(x)
takes
\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
time. Otherwise, it may take
\BigO((1+\log\frac{W}{w(x)})\cdot(\log\ell_{\max}+\log\log W))
time. Finding the
i
th node takes
\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
time.
∎

Claim 33
.

Replacing the set of light children of a node
x
with a new specified set of light children of the same weight takes
\BigO(1)

time.
If their weights differ, and
x
is an ancestor of a selected node, it takes time
\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
.

In the special case where
x
is an isolated node, it again takes
\BigO(1)
time.

Proof.

If there are no changes to the weights, this is local operation, changing only a constant number of pointers.

If
x
is an ancestor of a selected node, it has constant light depth, and it has constant depth in the at most one light structure to which it belongs. Thus, the cost is incurred by the at most two heavy paths, each yielding at most an additive
\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
term.

Finally, if
x
is an isolated node, there is nothing else to update, and it takes constant time.
∎

Claim 34
.

The procedures
\textsc{UnselectHeavy}(x)
and
\textsc{SelectHeavy}(x)
work in the time complexity
\BigO(\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max})
.

Proof.

If
y
is a heavy child of
x
then either
y
is an ancestor of a selected node or
2w(T_{y})>w(T_{x})
. In any case, as discussed earlier, the depth of it in the respective biased disjoint sets data structures is
\BigO(\log\log W)
, hence these procedures work in the time complexity
\BigO(\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max})
.
∎

Note, finally, that the analysis annotated in the pseudocode counts the unitary operations in the data structure (i.e., it assumes that the leaves hold single values instead of bit vectors and counter vectors). To obtain the final analysis of the actual running time, one in fact has to take into account that we not only perform operations on one number, but on a vector of size
\ell_{\max}
. In other words, all the stated running times should be multiplied by a factor
T(\ell_{\max})
, denoting the time it takes to perform operations on a vector of size
\ell_{\max}
.

function

Insert
(
x
)

\textsc{InsertAt}(x,\ell_{\max})

\triangleright

\BigO(\log\frac{W}{w(x)}+\ell_{\max}\cdot(\log\log W+\cdot\log\ell_{\max}))

(

)

i=\ell

downto

0

do

p
at level
i

\triangleright

\triangleright

\triangleright

\textsc{Link}(x,\mathrm{root})

\triangleright

\BigO(\log\frac{W}{w(x)}+\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max})

Algorithm 3

Level

function

(

)

cx\leftarrow

at level

\triangleright

Since
x\nsim_{i}y

.

, or
\mathcal{L}_{i-1}(x)

.

(

)

\triangleright
Both

and

i-1
to

\triangleright

(

)

\triangleright

\triangleright

\BigO(\log\frac{w(py)}{w(cy)}+\log\log W)

if

then

\triangleright

p_{x}
and

are both

-marked, so the cut and link here are cheap.

\triangleleft

\textsc{Cut}(cx)

\triangleright

\BigO(\log\frac{W}{w(cx)}+\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max})

\triangleright

\BigO(\log\frac{W}{w(cx)}+\log\log W+\log\ell_{\max})

5.1
Extensions

To handle the counting and marking extensions we need to augment the
nodes in our neighborhood tree, our heavy path trees, and our biased
disjoint set trees with some additional information.

For the heavy path trees, their leaves are nodes of the neighborhood tree and the counting (respectively, marking) information in an internal node is simply the sum (respectively, or) of the interval of leaves represented by the node.

For the biased disjoint sets counting (respectively, marking) is, again, simply a sum (respectively, or), of the values for the subset represented by the light set.
Furthermore, we also need to point to the ‘‘heaviest’’ element in the light set, and for this heaviest light child, store which selected nodes (if any) are below this child.

6
Biased Disjoint Sets (BDS) structure

In this section, we prove
Theorem

5
, that is, we provide an efficient implementation of the Biased Disjoint Sets data structure, therefore completing the stack of reductions required for proving
Theorem

1
.

Our structure is inspired by Binomial Heaps and Fibonacci Heaps
[
36
,
11
]
: we maintain, for each set
X
currently in the collection, a partition of
X
into at most
t\leq 2\log_{2}w(X)
subsets
X_{1},\ldots,X_{t}
, represented by a perfectly biased binary tree for each
X_{i}
(which we call
lower trees
).
These lower trees are then combined into a single tree using a simple balanced dynamic binary tree (e.g. AVL or red-black tree), which we call the
upper tree
; we require the depth of an
\ell
-leaf upper tree to be
\BigO(\log\ell)
, and we require that two upper trees, with
\ell_{a}
and
\ell_{b}
leaves each, can be joined into one balanced upper tree in worst-case time
\BigO(\log(\ell_{a}+\ell_{b}))
.
Each leaf of the upper tree is identified with the root of a lower tree.
Since the upper tree is balanced, it is guaranteed to have height
\BigO(\log t)=\BigO(\log\log w(X))
.
We call the resulting tree structure an
encoding
of
X
.
We associate the potential value
t
with this encoding.

Each node of a lower tree is assigned an integer
rank
.
The rank of a leaf representing a single element
x
is
\left\lfloor\log_{2}w(x)\right\rfloor
.
In turn, a non-leaf node of rank
i
has two children of rank
i-1
.
This way, each lower tree is perfectly biased, and in particular, the height of a lower tree representing a subset
X_{i}
of
X
is at most
\log_{2}w(X_{i})
.
Also, for
x\in X_{i}
, the depth of
x
in the lower tree is (by definition)
\BigO\left\lparen\log\frac{w(X_{i})}{w(x)}\right\rparen
.
Thus, the depth of any
x\in X
in the encoding of
X
is
\BigO\left\lparen\log\frac{w(X)}{w(x)}+\log\log w(X)\right\rparen
, as required by the definition of almost perfectly biased tree.

For an encoding of a set
X
, we maintain the bound on the potential value
t
by partitioning according to rank.
Whenever an encoding of
X
holds more than
2\log_{2}w(X)
lower trees, we perform a
simplification
of the encoding by combining lower trees: whenever we have two perfectly biased trees of rank
i
, we can combine them into one perfectly biased tree of rank
i+1
.
This process is applied whenever the encoding holds multiple lower trees of the same rank; therefore, when nothing more can be combined, we will hold at most
\log_{2}w(X)
different lower trees.
Finally, the new encoding of
X
is formed by joining the resulting lower trees into an encoding via a balanced upper tree.
Observe that this simplification process can be performed in time
\BigO(t)
and decreases the potential value of the encoding by at least
t-\log_{2}w(X)\geq\frac{1}{2}t
.
Therefore, in the amortized setting, simplification of an encoding can be performed “for free”: the drop in potential caused by this merging pays for the work done (because all additional work is paid for by the drop in potential).

Consider now the operations.
We implement
\textsc{Find}(x)
by following the tree encoding of the set
X\ni x
from the leaf corresponding to
x
to the root of the encoding of
X
.
This trivially can be done in worst-case time
\BigO\left\lparen\log\frac{w(X)}{w(x)}+\log\log w(X)\right\rparen
.

For
\textsc{RootUnion}(X,Y)
, the direct cost is just the cost of joining the two upper trees, which is
\BigO(\log\log(w(X)+w(Y)))
. However, this may lead to the resulting encoding having too many lower trees. When this happens, we perform the simplification of the encoding as described above.
This way, the amortized cost of
RootUnion
is the same as its actual cost.

The most complicated operation is
\textsc{Delete}(x)
. For
x\in X_{i}
, we delete the lower tree representing
X_{i}
from the upper tree. Then, we split this lower tree into
s=\BigO\left\lparen\log\frac{w(X_{i})}{w(x)}\right\rparen
smaller perfectly biased binary trees
X^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,X^{\prime}_{s}
by deleting all
s
ancestors of
x
. Then, we make a new upper tree with
X^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,X^{\prime}_{s}
as leaves. Finally, we perform a
RootUnion
on the two encodings: the original encoding with
X_{i}
removed and the new encoding of
X^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,X^{\prime}_{s}
. Note that both the direct cost and the amortized cost of the entire operation is
\BigO\left\lparen s+\log\log w(X)\right\rparen
.

It remains to observe that both
\textsc{Coalesce}(x,y)
and
\textsc{Union}(x,y)
can be implemented in terms of a constant number of invocations to
MakeSet
,
RootUnion
,
Find
, and
Delete
.
It can then be easily verified that both the direct and the amortized cost of both operations is then
\BigO\left\lparen\log\frac{w(X)}{w(x)}+\log\frac{w(Y)}{w(y)}+\log\log(w(X)+w(Y))\right\rparen
.

7
Approximate counting

In this section we analyze the function
T(\ell_{\max})
representing the maximum cost of a single operation on a counter vector.
We will use the method of
approximate counting
introduced in a work of Thorup
[
35
]
and subsequently used in several results on dynamic graph connectivity
[
25
,
27
]
to show that for the purposes of the biconnectivity data structure, we can choose
T(\ell_{\max})\in\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
in the word RAM model.
Since
\ell_{\max}\in\BigO(\log n)
, we will conclude that operations on counter vectors can be performed in worst-case time
\BigO(\log\log n)
.
Note that the result of this section is completely analogous to its counterpart in the work on the dynamic maintenance of
2
-edge-connected components of a graph
[
25
]
.

First, observe that in
Section

3
, one can replace the invariant (
\dagger
) with the following weaker statement, for some fixed
C\in(1,2)
:

(
\dagger
’)

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, biconnected components in graph
G_{i}
have at most
\lceil\frac{n}{C^{i}}\rceil
vertices.

This increases the maximum possible cover level
\ell_{\max}
of a non-tree edge slightly, from
\log_{2}n
to
\BigO(\log n)
.
On the other hand, this allows us to give a looser definition of
small
and
large
biconnected components of the graph
G_{i}
in the proof of
Lemma

13
: every biconnected component of
G_{i}
is declared
small
or
large
, but it must be declared as
large
if it has strictly more than
\lceil\frac{n}{C^{i+1}}\rceil
vertices and
small
if it has at most
\frac{1}{2}\lceil\frac{n}{C^{i}}\rceil
vertices.
Then, in order to correctly declare a component as small or large, it is enough to know the
multiplicative approximation

s^{\prime}
of the size
s
of the component: a positive real number
s^{\prime}\geq 1
such that
s^{\prime}\leq s\leq Bs^{\prime}
, where
B=2/C>1
.
We omit technical details here since completely analogous arguments appear in
[
35
,
25
]
.

Thus, following
[
35
]
, we implement counters as short
floating-point numbers
with a
b
-bit mantissa and an
s
-bit exponent, i.e., approximate positive integers with values of the form
(1+x\cdot 2^{-b})\cdot 2^{y}
for
x\in[0,2^{b})
,
y\in[0,2^{s})
.
Given two floating-point representations
r(\alpha)
,
r(\beta)
of positive integers
\alpha
,
\beta
, we can add them together and round down the result to the largest representable number, producing a representation
r(\alpha+\beta)
of
\alpha+\beta
.
Then whenever
\alpha+\beta<2^{2^{s}}
and both
r(\alpha)\leq\alpha\leq r(\alpha)\cdot K
,
r(\beta)\leq\beta\leq r(\beta)\cdot K
for some
K\geq 1
, we will have
r(\alpha+\beta)\leq\alpha+\beta\leq r(\alpha+\beta)\cdot K(1+2^{-b})
.
2
2

2

On the other hand, this representation prevents us from subtracting counters: notably, subtraction of floating-point representations of integers is prone to the risk of
catastrophic cancellation
.

This motivates the notion of the
computation depth
of a counter.
Each counter, on initialization with an integer, has computation depth
1
, and the sum of two counters of computation depths
d_{\alpha}
and
d_{\beta}
, respectively, has computation depth
\max\{d_{\alpha},d_{\beta}\}+1
.
Then the following simple fact is essentially argued in
[
35
,
25
]
:

Lemma 35

(
[
35
,
25
]
)
.

Fix
B>1
.
Then there exists a constant
A>0
, depending only on
B
, such that a counter of computation depth at most
A\cdot 2^{b}
storing a floating-point representation
r(\alpha)
of an integer
\alpha\in[1,2^{2^{s}})
satisfies
r(\alpha)\leq\alpha\leq r(\alpha)\cdot B
.

In the case of our biconnectivity data structure, we only use counters to find sizes of biconnected components (in terms of the number of vertices).
These never exceed
n
, so it is enough to choose
s\in\Theta(\log\log n)
.
Then, by
Lemma

35
, it is enough to pick some
b\in\Theta(\log d)
, where
d
is the maximum computation depth of any counter in the biconnectivity data structure.
The following claim estimates the value of
d
.

Lemma 36
.

The data structure preserves
d\in\log^{\BigO(1)}n
.

Proof.

We make a very rough estimate of
d
here, disregarding any optimizations stemming from the use of weights in the internal data structures; in the arguments below, we implicitly use the facts that these weights never exceed
\BigO(n)
, and that
\ell_{\max}\in\BigO(\log n)
.

In the BDS structure in
Section

6
, we maintain a binary tree of height
\BigO(\log n)
. Each node of the tree maintains a counter vector computed from its children within a constant number of operations, hence each counter has computation depth
\BigO(\log n)
.

By the same token, the neighborhood structure of
Section

5
maintains a balanced binary search tree of height
\BigO(\log n\log\log n)
, where each node stores
\BigO(1)
counter vectors and additionally an instance of the BDS structure.
The counters in a node are again easily found to be computable in
\BigO(1)
operations from the counters stored in the children in the binary tree and the counters kept in the root of the associated BDS structure; hence every counter in the neighborhood structure has computation depth
\log^{\BigO(1)}n
.

Finally, the cover level data structure is a top tree of height
\BigO(\log n)
, with each cluster storing a collection of counter vectors and matrices.
These counters are only recomputed on cluster merges, where they are the product of a constant number of operations on counter vectors and matrices stored in the children of the cluster and a single neighborhood structure.
Thus once again, all counters in the top tree have computation depth
\log^{\BigO(1)}n
.

Finally, the biconnectivity data structure consumes the counters provided by the cover level data structure as they are, and only compares them to thresholds of the form
\left\lceil\frac{n}{C^{i}}\right\rceil
.
Hence the lemma follows.
∎

So by
Lemma

36
we can choose
b\in\Theta(\log\log n)
, and therefore we implement counters as floating-point numbers with a representation of length
b+s\in\Theta(\log\log n)
.
Then using the standard bit-tricks exploiting the word RAM model
[
35
,
25
]
, we implement the
counter vectors
of length
\BigO(\log n)
by packing an array of floating-point representations of these counters into
\BigO(\log\log n)
RAM words (each of size
\Theta(\log n)
).
Hence we can choose
T(\ell_{\max})\in\BigO(\log\ell_{\max})
: all supported operations on counter vectors (see the list in
Section

2.1
) can be implemented in time
\BigO(\log\log n)
.

Appendix B
Implementation of the tree structure

B.1
Bookkeeping in top trees

A top trees data structure updates and propagates auxiliary information via the following callback functions:

•

\textsc{OnCreate}(C)
, called upon the creation of a leaf cluster
C
.
Recall that by our design choice, leaf clusters represent either single edges of the underlying tree
T
(call these
proper
leaf clusters), or dummy edges
vv^{\prime}
with one endpoint at a vertex
v\in V(T)
and the other at a fictional leaf
v^{\prime}
(
dummy
leaf clusters).

•

\textsc{OnMerge}(C)
, called when a cluster
C
is formed as a merge of several children of
C
.
The merge always happens according to one of the cases in
Figure

2
; and, by looking at
C
, we can infer which case holds and whether
C
is formed during a transient (un)expose.
After the merge, auxiliary information stored in
C
needs to be recomputed based on data stored in the children of
C
; and whenever
C
is formed outside of a transient (un)expose, weights and the sets of selected items in neighborhood data structures need to be updated.

•

\textsc{Clean}(C)
, called whenever lazy updates stored in
C
need to be propagated into child clusters.
This happens either just before
C
is split, or whenever recursive search along a vertical path of a top tree is performed (e.g., in
Section

4.6
).

In this section, we show how to maintain the correct values of
\mathrm{firstedge}_{C,x}
for
x\in\partial C
, the enclosing clusters
I_{x}
for
x\in V(T)
, the identification of cluster and interface edges (i.e., the sets
\mathrm{interface}_{x}
) and the correct edge and vertex weights.
Moreover, we construct neighborhood data structures
N_{x}
for
x\in V(T)
, where we preserve the correct weights and sets of selected edges incident to
x
.
Finally, for each cluster edge
\vec{uv}
, we maintain the smallest point cluster
\mathrm{smallestpoint}_{\vec{uv}}
containing the edge with the boundary equal to
u
(i.e., the smallest witness that
\vec{uv}
is indeed a cluster edge).

We refer to
Algorithm

21
for the implementation.
Here,
\mathrm{size}_{C}
describes the number of vertices in the cluster
C
, excluding the boundary vertices or dummy leaves of
T
.

B.2
Cover level data structure

We augment the previous data structure with the information on cover levels.
In the following algorithms, we assume that transient expose is invoked via
\textsc{TransientExpose}(v,w)
, and it returns three transient root clusters
R_{v},R_{w},R_{vw}
, such that
\partial R_{v}=\{v\}
,
\partial R_{w}=\{w\}
,
\partial R_{vw}=\{v,w\}
.
Then at the end of the query, the top tree must be transiently unexposed by calling
\textsc{TransientUnexpose}(
), thus restoring the original shape of the data structure.

SumCounters
from
Lemma

25

function

CR.OnCreate
(
C
)

\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C}\leftarrow{\bf 0}

\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,x}\leftarrow{\bf 0}
for each
x\in\partial C

function

CR.GetDiagCnt
(
C
,
v
)
\triangleright
Computes
\mathbf{diagcnt}_{C,v}

{\bf A}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v}}\,:\,{\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}+1}\,:\,{\bf 0}\rrbracket

\triangleright

A_{j,i}=\mathrm{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v,j,i}\cdot[j\leq\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}]

{\bf B}\leftarrow\llbracket{\bf 0}\,:\,{\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}+1}\,:\,{\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v}}\rrbracket

\triangleright

B_{j,i}=\mathrm{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v,j,i}\cdot[j>\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}]

{\bf w}\leftarrow\mathrm{sum}({\bf A})

\triangleright

w_{i}=\sum_{j}A_{j,i}

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf B},{\bf w},\mathrm{cover}_{C})

\triangleright

M_{j,i}=B_{j,i}+w_{i}\cdot[j=\mathrm{cover}_{C}]

return

{\bf M}

function

CR.GetTotalCnt
(
C
,
v
)
\triangleright
Computes
\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C,v}

{\bf M}\leftarrow\textsc{CR.GetDiagCnt}(C,v)

{\bf w}\leftarrow\mathrm{uppersum}({\bf M})

\triangleright

w_{i}=\sum_{j\geq i}M_{j,i}

return

{\bf w}

function

CR.OnMerge
(
C
)

if

C=A\cup B
,
\partial C\eqqcolon\{v\}
,
\partial A\eqqcolon\{v,x\}
,
\partial B\eqqcolon\{x\}

then

\triangleright
Case
(2)

{\bf c}^{x}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})+{\bf 1}

e\leftarrow\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v}

\mathbf{totalcnt}_{A,v}\leftarrow\textsc{CR.GetTotalCnt}(A,v)

\mathbf{clustercnt}_{e}\leftarrow\mathbf{totalcnt}_{A,v}+[{{\bf c}^{x}}\,:\,{\mathrm{cover}_{A}+1}\,:\,{\bf 0}]

N_{v}.\textsc{UpdateCounters}(e,\mathbf{clustercnt}_{e})

else

if

C=A\cup B\cup P
,
\partial C\eqqcolon\{v,w\}
,
\partial A\eqqcolon\{v,x\}
,
\partial B\eqqcolon\{x,w\}
,
\partial P\eqqcolon\{x\}

then

\triangleright
Case
(3)

{\bf c}^{AB}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},\,\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\})+{\bf 1}

{\bf c}^{A}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})+{\bf 1}

{\bf c}^{B}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\})+{\bf 1}

{\bf c}^{A\setminus B}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\},\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\})

{\bf c}^{B\setminus A}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\},\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})

\ell_{AB}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{Level}(\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x})

\mathbf{totalcnt}_{C}\leftarrow\mathbf{totalcnt}_{A}+\mathbf{totalcnt}_{B}+{\bf c}^{AB}

for all

(\hat{v},\hat{A},\hat{B})\in\{(v,A,B),(w,B,A)\}

do

\mathbf{diagcnt}_{\hat{A},\hat{v}}\leftarrow\textsc{CR.GetDiagCnt}(\hat{A},\hat{v})

\mathbf{diagcnt}_{\hat{B},x}\leftarrow\textsc{CR.GetDiagCnt}(\hat{B},x)

r\leftarrow\mathrm{cover}_{\hat{A}}

{\bf M}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathbf{diagcnt}_{\hat{B},x}}\,:\,{\min\{r,\ell_{AB}\}}\,:\,{\mathbf{diagcnt}_{\hat{A},\hat{v}}}\rrbracket

{\bf u}\leftarrow\mathrm{sum}(\llbracket{\bf 0}\,:\,{\min\{r,\ell_{AB}\}}\,:\,{\mathbf{diagcnt}_{\hat{B},x}}\rrbracket)

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf M},{\bf u},\min\{r,\ell_{AB}\})

if

\ell_{AB}<r

then

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf M},{\bf c}^{\hat{A}},r)

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf M},{\bf c}^{\hat{B}\setminus\hat{A}},\ell_{AB})

else

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf M},{\bf c}^{AB},r)

\mathbf{diagcnt}^{\star}_{\hat{A},\hat{v}}\leftarrow{\bf M}

Algorithm 26

Maintaining counters in the top tree clusters

function

CR.FindSize
(
p
,
q
,
i
)

R_{p},R_{q},R_{pq}\leftarrow\textsc{TransientExpose}(p,q)

\mathrm{answer}_{p}\leftarrow 1+\left(N_{p}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{R_{pq},p}\})\right)_{i}

\mathrm{answer}_{q}\leftarrow 1+\left(N_{q}.\textsc{SumCounters}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{R_{pq},q}\})\right)_{i}

\mathrm{answer}_{pq}\leftarrow\mathrm{totalcnt}_{R_{pq},i}

\textsc{TransientUnexpose}(
)

return

\mathrm{answer}_{p}+\mathrm{answer}_{q}+\mathrm{answer}_{pq}

Algorithm 27

FindSize
query

B.4
Finding reachable vertices

Recall that we keep, for each vertex
v
of the tree, a bit vector
\mathbf{bmarks}_{v}
where
\mathbf{bmarks}_{v,i}
denotes whether
v
is
i
-marked.
Initially, all bit vectors are identically zero.
Moreover, we assume access to a copy of the neighborhood data structure
N_{v}
named
N^{+}_{v}
.
We assume that all updates to
N_{v}
performed in
Sections

B.1
and
B.2
are also applied to
N^{+}_{v}
.

We first augment
N_{v}
with methods
\textsc{FindMarked}(A,i)
and
\textsc{OrMarks}(A,i)
and
N^{+}_{v}
with
\textsc{FindStrongMarked}(A,i)
as described in
Lemmas

28
and
29
; see
Algorithm

28
.
Afterwards, we maintain bit vectors in the top tree clusters analogously to the counters in
Section

B.3
(
Algorithm

29
).
Next, we resolve the updates and queries in
Algorithms

30
and
31
, delaying the implementation of the refinement functions until
Algorithm

32
.

function

N_{v}
.OrMarks
(
A_{+}
,
A_{-}
)

{\bf a}\leftarrow{\bf 0}

for all

e\in A_{+}

do

{\bf c}_{e}\leftarrow N_{v}.\textsc{OrMarks}(e)

\triangleright
Provided by
Lemma

4

p\leftarrow-1

for all

f\in A_{-}

do

p\leftarrow N_{v}.\textsc{Level}(e,f)

{\bf a}\leftarrow{\bf a}\mathbin{\mathrm{or}}[{\bf 0}:p+1:{\bf c}_{e}]

return

{\bf a}

function

N_{v}
.OrMarks
(
A_{+}
)

return

N_{v}
.OrMarks
(
A_{+}
,
\emptyset
)

function

N_{v}
.FindMarked
(
A
,
i
)

for all

e\in A

do

\mathrm{cand}\leftarrow N_{v}.\textsc{FindMarked}(e,i)

\triangleright
Provided by
Lemma

4

if

\mathrm{cand}\neq\bot

then

return

\mathrm{cand}

return

\bot

function

N^{+}_{v}
.FindStrongMarked
(
A
,
i
)

for all

e\in A

do

\mathrm{cand}\leftarrow N^{+}_{v}.\textsc{FindMarked}(e,i+1)

\triangleright
Provided by
Lemma

4

if

\mathrm{cand}\neq\bot

then

return

\mathrm{cand}

return

\bot

Algorithm 28

OrMarks
,

and

and

function

FR.OnCreate
(
C
)

\mathbf{totalmarks}_{C}\leftarrow{\bf 0}

\mathbf{diagmarks}^{\star}_{C,x}\leftarrow{\bf 0}
for each
x\in\partial C

function

FR.GetDiagMarks
(
C
,
v
)
\triangleright
Computes
\mathbf{diagmarks}_{C,v}

{\bf A}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathbf{diagmarks}^{\star}_{C,v}}\,:\,{\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}+1}\,:\,{\bf 0}\rrbracket

\triangleright

A_{j,i}=\mathrm{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v,j,i}\wedge[j\leq\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}]

{\bf B}\leftarrow\llbracket{\bf 0}\,:\,{\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}+1}\,:\,{\mathbf{diagmarks}^{\star}_{C,v}}\rrbracket

\triangleright

B_{j,i}=\mathrm{diagcnt}^{\star}_{C,v,j,i}\wedge[j>\mathrm{cover}^{\mathrm{top}}_{C}]

{\bf w}\leftarrow\mathrm{sum}({\bf A})

\triangleright

w_{i}=\bigvee_{j}A_{j,i}

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf B},{\bf w},\mathrm{cover}_{C})

\triangleright

M_{j,i}=B_{j,i}\,\operatorname{or}\,(w_{i}\wedge[j=\mathrm{cover}_{C}])

return

{\bf M}

function

FR.GetTotalMarks
(
C
,
v
)
\triangleright
Computes
\mathbf{totalmarks}_{C,v}

{\bf M}\leftarrow\textsc{FR.GetDiagMarks}(C,v)

{\bf w}\leftarrow\mathrm{uppersum}({\bf M})

\triangleright

w_{i}=\bigvee_{j\geq i}M_{j,i}

return

{\bf w}

function

FR.OnMerge
(
C
)

if

C=A\cup B
,
\partial C\eqqcolon\{v\}
,
\partial A\eqqcolon\{v,x\}
,
\partial B\eqqcolon\{x\}

then

\triangleright
Case
(2)

{\bf c}^{x}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{OrMarks}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})\mathbin{\mathrm{or}}\mathbf{bmarks}_{x}

e\leftarrow\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,v}

\mathbf{totalmarks}_{A,v}\leftarrow\textsc{FR.GetTotalMarks}(A,v)

\mathbf{ismarked}_{e}\leftarrow\mathbf{totalmarks}_{A,v}\mathbin{\mathrm{or}}[{{\bf c}^{x}}\,:\,{\mathrm{cover}_{A}+1}\,:\,{\bf 0}]

N_{v}.\textsc{UpdateMarks}(e,\mathbf{ismarked}_{e})

else

if

C=A\cup B\cup P
,
\partial C\eqqcolon\{v,w\}
,
\partial A\eqqcolon\{v,x\}
,
\partial B\eqqcolon\{x,w\}
,
\partial P\eqqcolon\{x\}

then

\triangleright
Case
(3)

{\bf c}^{AB}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{OrMarks}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},\,\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\})\mathbin{\mathrm{or}}\mathbf{bmarks}_{x}

{\bf c}^{A}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{OrMarks}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})\mathbin{\mathrm{or}}\mathbf{bmarks}_{x}

{\bf c}^{B}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{OrMarks}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\})\mathbin{\mathrm{or}}\mathbf{bmarks}_{x}

{\bf c}^{A\setminus B}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{OrMarks}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\},\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\})

{\bf c}^{B\setminus A}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{OrMarks}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\},\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\})

\ell_{AB}\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{Level}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\})

\mathbf{totalmarks}_{C}\leftarrow\mathbf{totalmarks}_{A}\mathbin{\mathrm{or}}\mathbf{totalmarks}_{B}\mathbin{\mathrm{or}}{\bf c}^{AB}

for all

(\hat{v},\hat{A},\hat{B})\in\{(v,A,B),(w,B,A)\}

do

\mathbf{diagmarks}_{\hat{A},\hat{v}}\leftarrow\textsc{FR.GetDiagMarks}(\hat{A},\hat{v})

\mathbf{diagmarks}_{\hat{B},x}\leftarrow\textsc{FR.GetDiagMarks}(\hat{B},x)

r\leftarrow\mathrm{cover}_{\hat{A}}

{\bf M}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathbf{diagmarks}_{\hat{B},x}}\,:\,{\min\{r,\ell_{AB}\}}\,:\,{\mathbf{diagmarks}_{\hat{A},\hat{v}}}\rrbracket

{\bf u}\leftarrow\mathrm{sum}(\llbracket{\bf 0}\,:\,{\min\{r,\ell_{AB}\}}\,:\,{\mathbf{diagmarks}_{\hat{B},x}}\rrbracket)

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf M},{\bf u},\min\{r,\ell_{AB}\})

if

\ell_{AB}<r

then

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf M},{\bf c}^{\hat{A}},r)

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf M},{\bf c}^{\hat{B}\setminus\hat{A}},\ell_{AB})

else

{\bf M}\leftarrow\mathrm{addvector}({\bf M},{\bf c}^{AB},r)

\mathbf{diagmarks}^{\star}_{\hat{A},\hat{v}}\leftarrow{\bf M}

Algorithm 30

Marking updates

function

FR.FindInPath
(
C
,
v
,
w
,
i
)

assert

C=A\cup B\cup P
,
\partial C=\{v,w\}
,
\partial A\eqqcolon\{v,x\}
,
\partial B\eqqcolon\{x,w\}
,
\partial P\eqqcolon\{x\}

\triangleright
Case
(3)

Clean
(
C
)
\triangleright
Push down any pending lazy updates in
C

if

\mathrm{totalmarks}_{A,i}

then

return

FR.FindInPath
(
A
,
v
,
x
,
i
)

else

if

\mathrm{bmarks}_{x,i}

then

return

(\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},x,i)

else

if

f_{A}\coloneqq N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\},i)\neq\bot

then

return

(\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x},x,f_{A})

else

if

f_{B}\coloneqq N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x}\},i)\neq\bot

then

return

(\mathrm{firstedge}_{B,x},x,f_{B})

else

assert

\mathrm{totalmarks}_{B,i}

return

FR.FindInPath
(
B
,
x
,
w
,
i
)

function

FR.FindFirstReach
(
p
,
q
,
i
)

R_{p},R_{q},R_{pq}\leftarrow\textsc{TransientExpose}(p,q)

\mathrm{answer}\leftarrow(\bot,\bot,\bot)

if

f\coloneqq N_{p}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{R_{pq},p}\},i)\neq\bot

then

\mathrm{answer}\leftarrow(\mathrm{firstedge}_{R_{pq},p},p,f)

else

if

\mathrm{totalmarks}_{R_{pq},i}

then

\mathrm{answer}\leftarrow\textsc{FR.FindInPath}(R_{pq},p,q,i)

else

if

f\coloneqq N_{q}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{R_{pq},q}\},i)\neq\bot

then

\mathrm{answer}\leftarrow(\mathrm{firstedge}_{R_{pq},q},q,f)

\triangleright

Now
\mathrm{answer}
is either negative
(\bot,\bot,\bot)
or positive (of the form
(ab,c,Y)
, with
Y
a container for
y
).

\triangleleft

if

(ab,c,Y)\coloneqq\mathrm{answer}\neq(\bot,\bot,\bot)

then

y\leftarrow

FR.RefineContainer
(
Y
,
i
)
\triangleright
Phase
2
(
Algorithm

32
)

\mathrm{answer}\leftarrow(ab,c,y)

\textsc{TransientUnexpose}(
)

return

\mathrm{answer}

function

FR.FindStrongReach
(
p
,
q
,
e
,
b
,
i
)

\textsc{TransientExpose}(p,q)

\mathrm{answer}\leftarrow N^{+}_{b}.\textsc{FindStrongMarked}(\{e\},i)

if

\mathrm{answer}
is a container
Y

then

\mathrm{answer}\leftarrow

FR.RefineContainer
(
Y
,
i
)
\triangleright
Phase
2
(
Algorithm

32
)

\textsc{TransientUnexpose}(
)

return

\mathrm{answer}

function

,

)

\mathrm{ismarked}_{\vec{vw},i}

C\leftarrow\mathrm{smallestpoint}_{\vec{vw}}

assert

C=A\cup B
,
\partial C=\{v\}
,
\partial A\eqqcolon\{v,x\}
,
\partial B\eqqcolon\{x\}

\triangleright
Case
(2)

if

\mathrm{totalmarks}_{A,v,i}

then

return

FR.RefinePathClusterContainer
(
A
,
v
,
x
,
i
)

assert

\mathrm{cover}_{A}\geq i

if

\mathrm{bmarks}_{x,i}

then

return

x

f\leftarrow N_{x}.\textsc{FindMarked}(\{\mathrm{firstedge}_{A,x}\},i)

assert

f\neq\bot

return

FR.RefineClusterEdgeContainer
(
f
,
i
)

(

function

FR.RefineContainer
(

,

)

y

,
i
)

else

,

,

,

Algorithm 32

Cluster refinement
